all keys R0D3, C4D3 and B01D3 ^Ob5qSNiW

def function(board: List[List[str]]) return boolean {
    hasSeen = {}
    for i in range(0,9) {
        for j in range(0,9) {
            if board[i][j] is not equal to "." {
                // key for row 
                rowKey = f"R{i}D{arr[i][j]}" // formatted string in python
                columnKey = f"C{j}D{arr[i][j]}"
                boxKey = f"B{i//3}{j//3}D{arr[i][j]}"
                if (rowKey or columnKey or boxKey) in hasSeen {
                    return false
                }else {
                    hasSeen[rowKey] = True
                    hasSeen[columnKey] = True
                    hasSeen[boxKey] = True
                }
                
            }
        }
    }
    return true    
} ^KFvYaGNG

Time Complexity
1. We have nested loop so without any further explanation first instinct is O(N^2)
2. Why since for each row we traverse all columns so For N X M matrix where N is no of rows and
M is no of columns we have O(N^2)
3. For each non-value entry we perform operations like key creation, checking and storing 
as Per hash Table checking is O(1), storing key is also O(1) on average. so for  keys 
it would 3 x O(1) so almost O(1)

I think O(N^2) would be the time Complexity

Space Complexity
1. We are storing booleans so it can be constant for values but since storing string of varying size 
like size 4 in row and column and size 5 in box so its O(N) space comlexity for keys 
Over all in worst cae we store 9x9 = 81 into 3 = 243 keys which is max keys we store
so i think O(N)  ^GvytUat0

feedback ^LZahA0MI

Time Complexity ✅
Your reasoning is mostly right, but let's be more precise:
For a general N×M matrix: Yes, O(N×M) or O(N²) if square.
But for this specific problem:

The board is always 9×9 (fixed size)
You traverse 81 cells (constant)
For each cell: O(1) operations (3 key checks + 3 insertions, all O(1))

So technically: O(9×9) = O(81) = O(1) constant time!
Since the input size never changes (always 9×9), it's constant time. But if we generalize to an N×N Sudoku board, then yes, O(N²) is correct.
Answer: O(1) for standard Sudoku, or O(N²) for N×N board ✅

Space Complexity - Let's Reconsider 🤔
You said: "O(N) for keys, 243 keys max, so O(N)"
Let's be more precise:

Maximum keys stored: 81 cells × 3 keys/cell = 243 keys (you got this!)
Key size: Each key is a string like "R0D3" (4-5 chars). This is constant size, not varying with N.
For standard 9×9 Sudoku: 243 keys of constant size = O(1) space
For N×N Sudoku: Maximum N² cells × 3 keys = 3N² keys = O(N²) space

Your conclusion "O(N)" isn't quite right. It should be:

O(1) for fixed 9×9 board
O(N²) for generalized N×N boar ^RSvk5jqf
